No. l-CA(75)/75. — In exercise of the powers conferred 
by sub section (1) of Section 30 of the Chartered Accoun- 
tants Act, 1949 (XXXVIII of 1949), the Council of the 
Institute of Chartered Accountants of India has made the 
following amendments to the Chartered Accountants Regula- 
tions, 1964, the same having been previously published and 
approved by the Central Government as required under sub- 
section (3) of the said section. 

In the said Regulations - 

1. In regulation 32A, delete sub-regulation (2) and its 
proviso. 

2. In regulation 34B, for existing sub-regulation (4), 
substitute' the following 

1‘(4) If the statement.' mentioned in sub-regulation 
(3) above is not received within the time specified, 
the , Secretary may condone tho delay where the mem- 
ber proves to his satisfaction (hat he was prevented 
from scaling th; statement in time, if he receives the 
same from Ihc member between the 16th and the 
30th day from the date of commencement of service, 
failing which the Secretary shall treat the date of 
commencement of service as the 16th day prior to its 
receipt by him. If the date of commencement of 
service is changed by the Secretary, he shall communi- 
cate such change to the member who shall rnako 
appropriate changes in the articles. 

3. In regulation 48B, delete sub-regulation (4) and its 
proviso. 

4. In regulation 48R, for the existing sub-regulation (5), 
substitute the following 

“(5) If the application for registration mentioned in 
sub-regulation (2) above is not received within, the 
time specified, the Secretary may condone the delay 
where the member proves to his satisfaction that be 
was prevented from sending the particulars in time, if 
he receives the application from the member between 
the 1 6th and 30th day from tho date of commence- 
ment of service, failing which the Secretary shall treat 
the date of commencement of service as tho 16th day 
prior to its receipt; by him. If the date of commence- 
ment of service is changed by the Secretary, he shall 
communicate such change to the member.” 

P. S. GOPALAKRISHNAN, Sccy.

No. 1-CA(95 ) /75. — The following draft of certain amend- 
ments to ihe Chartered Accountants Regulations, 1964, which 
it is proposed to make in exercise of the powers conferred by 
sub-sections (1) and (3) of Section 30 of the Chartered 
Accountants Act, 1949 (Act. XXXVIU of 1949), is published 
for information of all persons likely to he affected thcrcbv 
and notice is hereby given that the draft will be taken up 
for consideration on or after the 31.tr January 1976. 

Any objeclion or suggestion which may he received from 
any person with respect to the said draft before the date 
specified will be considered by the Council of Ihe Institute 
of Chartered Accountants of 1 India, New Delhi. 

In the said Regulations : — 

I. Tn Regulation 65. add the following new .sub-regulations 
at the end : — 

“(3) The nomination shall be accompanied by a statement 
signed and verified by the candidate containing information 
concerning the candidate in respect of Ihe following matters 
and shall not be valid unless it is so accompanied : — 

(a) Name, membership no. and address (as published 
in the List of Voters) 

(b) Agg 


Cc) Academic qualifications (diplomas and degrees re- 
cognised by Government and membership of re- 
cognised professional bodies) 

(d) Whether associate or fellow 

(e) Year of enrolment — ns an associate — as a fellow.’’ 

‘‘(4) The Statement referred lo in sub-regulation (3) 
above may also contain, at the option of the candidate, in- 
formation concerning ihe candidate in respect of the follow- 
ing matters : — 

(a) Passport size recent photograph 

(b) Merit awards in Ihe examinations ot recognised 
universities and ihe examinations conducted by (he 
Institute 

(c) Particulars of occupation — 

(i) employment (name of employer with designation) 

(ii) pmclioe (sole proprietor or in partnership in- 
cluding the name of the firm) 

(in) particulars of other occupation, if not covered 
by (i) and (ii) above 

(d) other particulars — 

(i) past and present membership of Central Council, 
Regional Councils and Managing Committees of 
branches of Regional Councils, including 
office of President and /or Vice-President in the 
case of Central Council and office of Chairman, 
Vice-Chairman. Secretary and/or Treasurer in 
tho case of Regional Councils and/or branches 
of Regional Councils 

(ii) contributions in professional seminars and con- 
ferences wilhin a period of five years before 
the date of nomination 

(iii) authorship of books on subjects of professional 
interest 

(iv) authorship of articles on professional subjects 
not covered by (ii) & (iih above within a 
period of five years before the date of nomi- 
nation 

(v) grant, of National and State awards 

(vi) academic positions held in recognised universities 

(vii) office held as Vice-Chairman, Secretary and/or 
Treasurer in the case of the Managing Com- 
mittee of a Students’ Association of Ihc .Institute 
or its branch /cs.”

the end : — ■

in Ihc List of Voters')

OUTLINE OF THE INDUSTRIAL FINANCE CORPORA- 
TION OF INDIA 

Incorporation and Purpose 

The Industrial Finance Corporation of India (IFCI) was 
established in 1948 under an Act of the Indian Parliament 
with the object of making medium and long-term credits to. 
industrial concerns in India. 



602 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA, JANUARY 10, 1976 (PAUSA 20, 1897) [Part III— Sec 4 


Capital 

The authorised capital of ITC.I is Rs. 20 crorcs. Fifty per 
cent of the paid-up capital now standing at Rs. 10 crores 
is held by the Industrial Development Bank of India (LDBi). 
The remaining 50% is held by scheduled banks, cooperative 
banks, insurance concerns and investment trusts, etc. 

Management 

The Board of Directors consists of a wholetinie Chairman 
and twelve directors. The Chairman is appointed by the 
Central Government after consultation with IDBT Two 
directors are nominated bv the Central Government and four 
by 1DBI. .Six directors are elected by shareholders other than 
IDBI. 

I unctions and Lending Policies 

Any limited company or cooperative society incorporated and 
registered in India which is engaged, or proposes to engage 
itself, in the manufacture, preservation or processing of goods, 
or in the shipping, mining or hotel industry or in the genera- 
tion or distribution of electricity or any other form of power, 
is eligible for, financial assistance. 

SUMMARY OF OPERATIONS 


Public sector projects are also eligible for financial assist- 
ance from Ihe Corporation on the same basis as industrial 
projects in the private sector. 

Assistance may take the form of long-term loans — -both in 
rupees and foreign currencies, underwriting of equity, pre- 
ference and debenture issues; subscribing to equity, prefer- 
ence and debenture capital; guaranteeing of deferred pay- 
ments in respect of machinery imported from abroad or 
purchased in India, and guaranteeing of loans raised in 
foreign currency from foreign financial institutions. Financial 
assistance from the Corporation is available for the setting-up 
of new industrial projects as also for the expansion, diversi- 
fication, renovation or modernisation of existing ones. 

Financial assistance, on concessional terms, is available for 
setting up industrial projects in certain industrially less deve- 
loped districts in the States/Union Territories notified by the 
Central Government. 

Sources of Funds 

Thg main sources of funds of the Corporation other than its 
own capital, retained earnings, repayment of loans and sale 
of investments, arc borrowings from th e market bv the issue; 
of bonds, loans from the Central Government and foreign 
credits.

THE YEAR IN BRIEF 

The operations of the Corporation during the year have 
to be viewed in the context of the steep rise in the capital 
cost of new projects on priority industries the depressed state 
of the capitai market and the severe constraint faced by the 
Corporation in regard to its resources. These factors neces- 
sitated a cautious and selective approach on the part of the 
Corporation towards undertaking fresh commitments. 

Sanctions 

Despite its diflicull resources position, the Corporation was 
able to sanction, during the year ended June 30, 1975 net 
financial assistance of Rs, 36.86 crores spread over 100 pro- 
jects. The projects assisted during the year were -estimated 
to cost Rs. 486.66 crores and were spread over 16 States and 
two Union Territories. They included 49 new projects which 
claimed 72% of the net sanctions. 

Some of the important indstries which were sanctioned 
assistance during the year by the Corporation were fertilisers, 
textiles, sugar, paper, cement, iron and steel, automobile tyres 
and lubes and basic industrial chemicals. 

Sector-w ise A ssis hi nee 

Nine projects in the cooperative sector, including seven 
sugar projects, were sanctioned assistance amounting to Rs. 4.90 
crores which formed 13.3% of the total net sanctions. 
Fourteen projects in the joint sector and four in the public 
sector were sanctioned assistance of Rs. 5.73 crores and 
Rs. 1.80 crores respectively and together accounted for 
20.4% of sanctions. Financial assistance sanctioned for 73 
projects in the private corporate sector amounted to Rs. 24.43 
crores. 

Projects in Less Developed / {reus 

45 projects located in the notified less developed district/ 
areas were assisted by the Corporation during the year and the 
net assistance of Rs. 19,95 crores sanctioned for these projects 
represented 54.1% of the total sanctions. As on June 30. 
1975, in all, 217 projects located in notified less developed 
districts/arcas stood covered by sanctions over the years 
amounting to Rs. 157-93 crores. 

Disbursements 

Financial assistance disbursed during the year amounted to 
Rs. 37.42 crores of which cash disbursements amounted to 
Rs. 37.08 crores. 

Bond Issues 

With a view to augmenting its resources, the Corporation 
made a Bond issue in June 1975, for Rs. 11,25 crores which 
was fully subscribed. Harlicr, in September 1974. the Corpo- 
ration made a cash -trim -con version Bond issue of Rs, 10 
crores which was also fully subscribed. 

Foreign Credits 

A further loan of DM 11.00 million from the Kreditgnstalt 
of West Germany was allocated to the Corporation during 
the year. , - 

Working Results 

The working results for the year disclosed a gross profit 
before tax of Rs. 4.59 crores. After making provision for 
taxation to the extent of Rs. 1.99 crores (net), the net profit 
was Rs. 2.60 crores. Net additions made to reserves amount- 
ed to Rs. 1 .70 crores, bringing the total reserves of the Cor- 
poration to Rs. 22.13 crores which exceeded the paid-up capi- 
tal by Rs. 12.13 crores. 

Risk Capital Foundation 

The Corporation sponsored the Risk Capital Foundation 
which was registered in New Delhi as a Society in January 
1975 under the Societies Registration Act, 1860. Th c main 
objective of the Foundation as envisaged would be to provide 
assistance to new entrepreneurs, particularly technologists and 
professionals for promoting industrial projects by giving soft 
loans to enable them to provide a part of equity capital as 


promoters’ contribution- to the cost of projects which have 
been sanctioned financial assistance by the Industrial Finance 
Corporation of India alone or jointly with other all-India 
financial institutions. Tile Foundation was inaugurated by 
Shri T. A. Pai, Minister of Industry and Civil Supplies on 
March 10, 1975. 

Management Development Institute 

The Management Development Institute (MDi) sponsored 
by the Corporation started conducting courses from March 

1974. Till December 31, 1974, MDI had organised 14 courses 
in fields like general management, personnel management, 
marketing management, financial management, ns also in the 
field of development banking and in the areas of manage- 
ment of specific industries besides one programme for the 
training of the Corporation’s staff. During the first half of 

1975, MDi conducted ten programmes and is conducting 16 
more courses during the latter half of the year. 

I PUP Programme 

The Corporation is sponsoring training programmes on 
‘Identification, Promotion and Implementation .of Industrial 
Projects’ (1PI1P) for the benefit of officials in the State 
Government and State-level developmental agencies as also 
entrepreneurs. These programmes are being conducted on 
behalf of the Corporation by MDI. The first such program- 
me was organised a-.’ Puri in Orissa in February this year 
and had an encouraging response. 

Second IFCl Silver Jubilee Memorial Lecture 

'the Second 1FCI Silver Jubilee Memorial Lecture was 
delivered by Mr. William Diamond. Director, South Asia 
Department, Internalional Bank for Reconstruction and Deve- 
lopment, Washington on October 16, J974. The subject of 
Mr. Diamond’s Lecture was “Intcr-play between Financial 
insfilutions and Economic Development,” The proceedings at 
the function organised in connection with the Lecture have 
been published. 


Report of thc Board of Directors for (he year ended June 
30, 1973 under Section 33 of the Industrial Finance Corpora- 
tion Act, 1948. (15 of 1948) 

THE YEAR REVIEWED 

The Board of Directors present herewith their Twenty- 
Seventh Report on the operations of the Corporation, together 
with thc audited Statement of Accounts, for the year ended 
June 30. 1975. 

2. The operalions of thc Corporation during the year have 
to be viewed in the context of the steep rise in the capital 
cost of new projects which has been in evidence during the 
past two years. All immediate consequence of this develop- 
ment has been a marked increase in the quantum of finances 
required for implementing projects. This in turn meant that 
not only the entrepreneurs had to put in greater efforts to 
mobilise resources of their own but there was also a larger 
demand on thc financial institutions for funds. This situa- 
tion was accentuated by the relatively depressed state of the 
capital market \Vhich ugain meant increasing recourse to the 
institutions for underwriting support. On the other hand, the 
financial institutions themselves were faced with a severe con- 
straint in regard to their resources. Faced with an unprece- 
dented demand for funds, the Corporation had to be guided by 
certain priorities in selecting projects for assistance, consistent 
with its role as a development bank geared to the tasks of 
implementing national plans and socially desirable objectives. 
The Corporation endeavoured to channel assistance to indus- 
tries in th e priority sectors, and accorded preference to pro- 
jects located in less developed areas, ventures promoted ]iy 
new entrepreneurs and technologists, and undertakings con- 
tributing to the manufacture of goods of mass consumption 
such as sugar and textiles, promoting exports, or import, sub- 
stitution, or offering larger employment opportunities etc. 

The increase in the capitul post of projects has also been 
causing concern inasmuch as it has a direct bearing on the 
commercial viability of new projects. This phenomenon was 
particularly noticeable in some of the priority industries like 



Part III— Sec. 4] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA, JANUARY 10, 19-76 (PAUSA 20, 1897) 605 


sugar, cotton .‘-.pinning, cement, paper and automobile tyres 
and tubes, and called for new approaches and discipline. 

Sanctions 

3. Gross financial assistance sanctioned by the Corporation 
during the year amounted to Rs. 37.91 crorcs. After cancel- 
lations, the net. financial assistance sanctioned amounted to 
Rs. 36.86 crorcs spread over 100 projects. The total cost of 
the projects assisted during the year was estimated at. 
Rs. 486.66 crorcs. The details of assistance sanctioned during 
the year for projects in a wide variety of industries, are given 
in Appendix A along with a brief description of the assisted 
projects. 

4. Assistance of Rs. 4.90 crorcs sanctioned for projects in 
the cooperative sector represented 13.3% of the total net 
sanctions. Project's in the joint sector and public sector were 
sanctioned assistance of Rs. 5.73 crores and Rs. 1.80 crorcs 
respectively. Table 1 gives the sector-wise, classification of 
financial assistance sanctioned during the year. 

5. New projects claimed 72.0% of the total net sanctions 
and expansion, modernisation and diversification projects 
17.9%. Projects involving additional assistance for meeting 
over-runs accounted for 10.1 % of total net sanctions. Of 
the projects assisted during the year, 49 were new, 17 were 
expansion/ diversification projects, 14 involved modernisation, 
rehabilitation etc., and in the case of the remaining 20 pro- 
jects additional assistance was sanctioned for meeting over- 
runs in (heir costs. 


Two new projects were sanctioned assistance for the manu- 
facture of carbon steel and mild steel billets, One of them 
viz.., Maharashtra Elektrosmelt Ltd., has been promoted by 
the .State Industrial and Investment Corporation of Maha- 
rashtra Ltd. and will manufacture carbon steel and mild steel 
billcts/ingots utilising iron ore deposits from the Lohana and 
Surajgarh mines in Maharashtra. The project costing 
Rs. 15.20 crorcs has an installed capacity of 64,000 tonnes per 
annum and is being set up at Chandrapur, a notified less 
developed district in Maharashtra. Another project promoted 
by the Andhra Pradesh Industrial Development Corporation 
(APIDCt envisages the manufacture of 47,000 tonnes per 
annum of rmld steel and high carbon steel billets on three 
shift basis. This joint sector project is being set up in Kham- 
mani, an industrially less developed district in Andhra Pradesh. 
The project has been designed to use sponge iron also as feed 
stock. 

Another project which was assisted by the Corporation 
during the year is the Hindustan Electro-Graphite Ltd- This 
project, which is being, set up in District Raiscn, a notified 
less developed district of Madhya Pradesh, envisages the 
manufacture of graphite electrodes with an installed capacity 
of 12,000 tonnes per annum on three shift basis. 

The Corporation sanctioned financial assistance for an 
expansion scheme of ACC-Vickers-Babcock Ltd., aimed at 
increasing its power boiler manufacturing capacity from 500 
MW to 1,000 MW ner annum. This increase in production 
capacity of power boilers would help in increasing the thermal 
power generation capacity. .

Review of Projects Assisted During the Year 

6. Some of the priority industries which were sanctioned 
assistance during the year by the Corporation were fertilisers, 
textiles, sugar, paper, cement, iron an t ) steel, automobile tyres 
and' lubes and basic industrial chemicals. 

The major beneficiary of the Corporation’s assistance during 
the year was the rubber products industry. Assistance to the 
extent of Rs. 5.16 crores was sanctioned for the setting-up of 
four new projects in this industry for the manufacture of auto- 
mobile tyres and tubes each with a capacity of 5 lakh number 
of automobile tyres and an equal number of tubes. These 
projects are bemn; set up in the notified less developed districts: 
one of them viz,., Tamil Nadu Rubber Ltd., is being set up in 
the joint sector. Assistance was also sanctioned for meeting 
the over-run of an automobile tyres and tubes project assisted 
earlier. 


A joint sector project being set up in Madras, Tamil Nadu 
has been sanctioned financial assistance for the manufacture, 
for the first time in the country, of biaxiallv oriented polys- 
tyrene films with an installed capacity of 1,000 tonnes ner 
annum. BOPs films are an essential input for the manufac- 
ture of capacitors. 

The Corporation sanctioned additional assistance to Manga- 
lore Chemicals and Fertilisers Ltd., an assisted concern, for 
its project being set up in the notified less developed district 
of South Kanara in . Karnataka. This project envisages the 
manufacture of ammonia and urea with an annual installed 
capacity of 2,17,800 tonnes and 3,40.000 tonnes respectively.

Application s for Assistance 

12. During the year, the Corporation received 115 applica- 
tions for financial assistance from 111 concerns, including 
applications involving, joint financing with other all-Tndia 
financial institutions. 

At the beginning of the year, application from 34 con- 
cerns seeking assistance to the extent of Rs. 150.96 crores, 
including applications which involved joint financing with 
other all-India financial institutions, were being processed by 
the Corporation. 

Gross financial assistance aggregating Rs. 37.91 
crores was sanctioned during the year by the Corporation in 
respect of 99 applications from 94 concerns. Applications 
from 3 concerns were treated as withdrawn and an applica- 
tion from one concern was rejected. 


At the end of the year, applications from 43 concerns 
for assistance aggregating Rs, 129.36 crores including appli- 
cations seeking assistance also from other all-India financial 
institutions, were under various stages of processing. The 
applications for assistance for new projects in the sugar and 
cotton spinning industries could not he considered for 
sanctioned by the Corporation jointly with other all-India, 
financial institutions, pending the creation of conditions 
establishing commercial viability of these units and the 
raising of the requisite initial share capital by the applicant 
concerns. 

A statement showing the particulars, Statewise, of appli- 
cations under processing at the beginning of the year, and 
applications received, sanctioned or withdrawn during the 
year as also applications under various stages of processing 
at the end of the year is given in Appendix ‘D’ to this 
Report.

OPERATIONAL DE VELOP M EN r rS 
Rate of interest 

13. Consequent to the increase in the Bank Rate from 
7% (since May 1973) to 9% in July 1974, the Corporation, 
along with other all-India term lending institutions, revised 
the rates of interest charged by it on rupee loans. Accord- 
ingly, the normal lending rate of interest charged by the 
Corporation from borrowing concerns Was raised from 9% 
to 10.25% (net) per annum. The rate of interest on rupee 
loans to industrial projects being set up in notified back- 
ward districts/areas, subject to certain conditions, was raised 
from 7.5% to 8-5% (net) per annum. Further, the rate 
of interest on soft loans to jute industry, export oriented 
cotton textile mills, and to the hotel industry was fixed 
at 9.25% (net) per annum as against 8,0% per annum in 
force earlier. 

The revision in the rate of interest was made applicable 
to all rupee loans sanctioned on or after July 30, 1974 as 
also ’to rupee loans sanctioned by the Corporation in res- 
pect of which the relative loan agreements had not been 
executed before July 30, 1974. 

The rates of interest charged by the Corporation from 
its assisted concerns on sub-loans in foreign currencies was 
also revised during the year. The rate of interest on foreign 
currency sub-loans was raised from 9.5% to 10.5% (net) 
per annum. The concessional rate of interest on foreign 
currency sub-loans charged to industrial projects being set 
up in notified backward districts/areas was raised by 0.5% 
to 9.5% (net) per annum. The revised rates of interest, 
have been made applicable to all sub-loans in foreign 
currencies including sub-loans in pound sterling sanctioned 
on or after August 29, 1974 as also to sub-loans sanctioned 
by the Corporation in respect of which loan agreements had 
not been executed by that date. 

Co-ordination with other all-India Financial Institutions 

14. The all-India financial institutions including the 
Corporation have evolved a common loan application form 
for use by eligible concerns seeking financial assistance from 
any one of them. The same form can be used even when 
assistance is sought jointly from more than one all-India 
financial institution. The institutions have adopted Ihe 
concept of the ‘lead institution’ whereby one of them 
takes the responsibility of arranging for a joint inspection 
of the plant site by the officials of all the institutions. 
Collective discussions are arranged with the promoters as 
also among the representatives of the institutions for evolv- 
ing a common approach towards various aspects of a 
project for which financial assistance is required, The 
institutions are further examining the feasibility of introduc- 
ing a standard Loan Agreement in the case of projects 
being jointly ftnAWccd by them. These steps would facilitate 
expeditious processing of applications for joint financing. 

Viability Studies of High-cost Protects 

15. As stated earlier in the Report, the steep increase in the 
costs of new projects has affected their commercial viability. 
In order to study the impact of the rise in the project cost of. 
unit of the minimum economic size, the Corporation under- 
took and completed viability studies in respect of new sugar 
projects with a crushing capacity of 1,250 tonnes of cane per 
day and new cotton spinning units with 25,000 spindles. The 
Corporation is at present carrying out a similar viability study 


of mini-paper plants. The findings of these studies under- 
taken by the Corporation will be considered by the all-India 
financial institutions with a view to evolving a common app- 
roach to the financing of new projects in die industries con- 
cerned. 

Officers of the Corporation 

16. The Branch Office of the Corporation at Delhi was 
raised to the status of Regional Office with effect from 
August 6, 1975. A new office of the Corporation was opened 
at Nagpur with effect from January 27, 1975. The Corpora- 
tion now has 17 offices all over the country besides its Head- 
Office. 

Brochures for Entrepreneurs 

17. The Advisory Services Department of the Corporation 
is bringing out a series of brochures which deal with the 
various aspects of setting up medium-sized industrial projects 
So far, two brochures in the series “How to set up a mediumt 
sized industry”, have been brought out viz., (n) Government 
Policies and Procedures; (b) Guidelines for Incorporation of 
a Limited Company. A third brochure entitled “Presenting 
Your Case for Financial Assistance” is under preparation. 
It is hoped that such brochures would be of benefit to new 
entrepreneurs, 

REVIEW OF OPERATIONS 1948—75 

18. Starting modestly. IFCl’s business has grown In size 
and complexity, and its operations have progressively increas- 
ed to keep pace with the tempo of industrialisation in the 
country, A significant feature of the Corporation’s opera- 
tions has been the integration of its lending and investment 
policies with the countrys Five Year Plans 

IFCI’s contribution to the economic development of the 
country should be judged not only by the amount of finan- 
cial assistance sanctioned or the 'number of projects assisted 
by it, but also by the effect this assistance nas had on the 
economy as a whole. Some of these benefits include the 
development of rural economy through assistance to indust- 
rial cooperatives, especially, in the sugar and textile industries; 
development of the less developed areas; increased employ- 
ment opportunities; development of new products and techno- 
logies; encouragement to new entrepreneurs and technolo- 
gists; increased availability of inputs for augmenting agricul- 
tural production, e.g. fertilisers, pesticides, agricultural machi- 
nery; encouragement to export-oriented and import-saving 
projects; modernisation of export-oriented jute and textile 
mills etc. Although the small scale and ancillary industries 
have not been the direct recipients of financial assistance 
from the Corporation, they have benefited indirectly, Finan- 
cial assistance from the Corporation has helped establish a 
iiumDer of large and medium-sized industries, which havo in 
turn helped the promotion and growth of ancillary and small 
scafe industries and contributed to the creation of national 
wealth and employment. 

The Corporation as on June 30, 1975 had financed 727 
large a-nd medium sized industrial projects spread all over 
the country with a total assistance amounting to Rs. 507.39 
crores. This assistance was extended to 126 concerns in the 
cooperative sector, 25 concerns in the joint sector, 13 con- 
cerns in the public sector and 490 in the private corporate 
sector. Disbursements amounted to Rs, 441.44 crores which 
represented 87,0% of sanctions. The total assistance out- 
standing as on June 30, 1975 amounted to Rs. 235.67 crores. 
More important, however, than the quantum of assistance 
sanctioned by IFCI, is its catalytic role which has led to re- 
source mobilisation of the order of Rs, 2881.66 crores for 
the completion of the assisted projects. In its assistance, the 
Corporation has covered a wide variety of industries. And, 
it is no exaggeration to say that there is hardly any industry 
of importance in the country which has not been the benefi- 
ciary of some assistance from IFCI. 

IFCT’s operations have been consistently profitable. JFCI’s 
sound financial position is reflected in the fact that it has 
been able to build up reserves, out of taxed profits which 
were substantially in excess of the paid-up share capital.

24, The extent to which saving in the agricultural sector 
have DCCn momusea for productive purposes as a result of the 
cataiyiic roie of ihe Corporation is evident from the fact that 
in respect of 77 cooperative sugar factories in operation 
(luring me iy7J-74 season, grower member and other had 
contributed a sum of Rs. 41.75 erorcs as share capital while 
contribution from the State Governments amounted to Rs. 
22.14 crores. In addition, assisted sugar cooperatives which 
had gone into production had mobilised refundable and non- 
refundable deposits, internal accruals etc., amounting to Rs. 
12.46 crores. The assistance sanctioned by the Corporation 
for the projects of these cooperatives amounted to 47.3 % 
of the total project cost of Rs, 160.55 crores. The assismjjce 
in the form of long term loans extended by the Life Insu- 
rance Corporation of India to these projects amounted to 
Rs. 8,25 crores and represented 5.1% of the, total project 
cost, 

25 . As stated last year in our Report, the cost of setting 
up a sugar factory with a crushing capacity of 1,250 tonnes 
per day has been increasing significantly. The cost was Rs. 
226 laths in 1969, Rs, 360 lakhs in 1973 and Rs. 530 laths 
in early 1974 and is now of the order of Rs. 700 laths. 
The implications of this development from the point 
of view of profitability and debt service capacity of such 
high cost units in the country were examined in the Corpo- 
ration and brought to the notice of Government. Govern- 
ment, thereafter appointed a Committee (called the Sampat 
Committee) to examine the question of the economic viability 
of new sugar projects in the light of the increased capital 
cost. The Committee has submitted its report and its re- 
commendations are under consideration of the Government. 

In view of the steep increase in the capital cost of new 
sugar projects and in the context of the constraint on the 
Corporation in regard to resources, the Corporation is no 
longer in a position to meet the higher loan requirements 
entirely from its own resources. It has now become neces- 
sary to adopt a consortium approach, whereby the other all- 
India financial institutions, besides the Life Insurance Corpo- 
ration of India which has been extended term loans for 
sugar projects, also meet a part of the loan requirement* of 
new sugar projects, 

THE CORPORATE SECTOR 

26. The financial assistance sanctioned to the corporate 
sector amounted to Rs. 391.53 crores in respect of 600 pro- 
ject* as on June 30, 1975. Details of assistance sanctioned 
to the corporate sector arc reviewed in the following para- 
graphs. 

Rupee Loans 

Rupee loan assistance sanctioned amounting to Rs. 237.15 
crores represented 60.6% of the total assistance to the cor- 
porate sector. The disbursement of rupee loans to the cor- 
porate sector as on June 30, 1975 amounted to Rs, 208.02 
crores. 


Foreign Currency Loans 

Foreign currency loans sanctioned by the Corporation to 
the corporate sector aggregated Rs. 58.13 crores, while dis- 
bursements amounted to Rs. 48.70 crores. 

Underwritings 

Upto June 30, 1975 the Corporation had sanctioned 400 
applications for underwriting of equity shares, preference 
shares and debentures for a net amount of Rs. 39.69 crores.

BENEVOLENT RESERVE FUND 

32. The IFC Act, 1948, as amended in December 1972, 
provided for the creation of the Benevolent Reserve Fund. 
With the creation of the Benevolent Reserve Fund two years 
ago it has been possible for the Corporation to undertake 
various promotional activities. As provided in Section 32B 
of the IFC Act, the Fund is being utilised for (a) meeting the 
cost of feasibility studies, project reports, market and techno- 
economic surveys, (b) assisting projects promoted by new 
entrepreneurs and technologists, (c) promoting research in 
financial and industrial management by creation of chairs in 
Universities and other academic institutions, (d) meeting the 
cost of training of personnel of financial institutions, etc. 
Since the creation of the Benevolent Reserve Fund a sum of 
Rs, 80.00 lakhs has been transferred to the Fund out of the 
orofits of the Corporation. A further sum of Rs, 31.00 lakhs 
is being transferred to the Fund out of the current years 
profits of the Corporation, 


33. The creation of the Benevolent Reserve Fund has given 
a new dimension to the promotional activities of the Corpo- 
ration. Some of the activities undertaken by the Corporation 
under the Fund arc reviewed in the following paragraphs. 

Industrial Potential Survey Reports 

34. The Corporation has participated in surveys o/ less 
developed States/Union Territories, sponsored by the Indus- 
trial Development Bank of India, with a view to assessing 
their industrial potential and for identifying projects which 
could be implemented over the short term. These surveys 
have been completed for all the 18 States /Union Territories 
identified as backward by the Pande Committec/Planning 
Commission viz., Andaman & Nicobar Islands, Andhra Pra- 
desh, Arunachal Pradesh, Assam, Bihar, Dadra and Nagar 
Haveli, Goa, Daman and Diu, Jammu and Kashmir, Hima- 
chal Pradesh, Lakshadweep, Madhya Pradesh, Manipur, Naga- 
land, Orissa, Pondicherry, Rajasthan, Tripura and Uttar 
Pradesh. 



Part III— Sec. 4] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA, JANUARY 10, 1976 (PAUSA 20, 1897) 615 


The survey reports, which have been printed, have been 
discussed with the concerned officials of the Slate Govern- 
ments and representatives of the State-level financial institu- 
tions and other industrial interests and projects ideas that 
seem prima facie viable have been identified. Follow-up 
action on the survey reports is being taken by Tnter-Institn- 
tional Groups (IIGs) established in the various States. The 
Corporation is taking an active interest in the functioning of 
the IIGs. 

Commissioning of Feasibility Studies 

35. Another important promotional measure undertaken bv 
the term lertding 'institutions is the commissioning of feasibi- 
lity reports on candidate projects identified in the afore-men- 
tioned industrial potential surveys. The cost of the feasibiliry 
reports is being shared atnong these institutions. These 
reports are commissioned with a .view to attracting the atten- 
tion of intending investors to the possibilities of the various 
Projects. As on Juno 30, 1975, 18 feasibility reports had 
been commissioned and these covered a Sugar Mill, a Hotel 
Project, a Jute Mill, a Steel Billets plant, a Granulated 
Fertiliser plant, a Solvent Extraction Unit, a Vegetable and 
Fruit Processing Unit, Central Processing and Powerlootns 
Projects (3 Reports), a Pulp and Paper Mill, a Calcium Car- 
bide Project, a Calcium Carbonate and Hydrated Lime Pro- 
mpt, a Cold Storage plant for Potatoes, an Asbestos Cement 
Sheets plant, a Meat Processing plant and tannery for hides 
and skins, a Sal seed Oil Project and a Cement Project. 

Technical Assistance Scheme 

36. The Corporation instituted a Technical Assistance 
Scheme last year. Under this Scheme, every month, two offi- 
cials at the middle and upper levels of the State-level financial 
and developmental agencies arc invited to visit the Head 
Office of the Corporation and its Regional Office in New 
Delhi for a period of one month. The Scheme also envisages 
visits of chief executives of the State-level financial and deve- 
lopmental agencies for a brief period to the Head Office of 
the Corporation. This Scheme gives an opportunity to the 
Corporation to share its experience in development banking 
with the officers of the State-level developmental agencies and 
also gives these officials an oooortunltv to study the policies 
procedures nd practices of the Corporation. 

Creation of Chairs 

37. In order to promote research in development banking 
and in financial and industrial management, the Corporation 
has created Chairs in the Indian Institute of Management, 
Ahmedabad and the University of Delhi by giving endow- 
ment of Rs. 7.00 lakhs and Rs. 8.00 lakhs respectively, The 
Indian Institute of Management, Ahmedabad has already 
appointed the IFCI Professor of Management and the Uni- 
versity of Delhi expects to find a suitable person for the 
Chair in the near future. 

RISK CAPITA! FOUNDATION 

38. In recent years, with a view to enlarging the entre- 
preneurial base in industry, Government has been laying in- 
creasing emphasis on encouragement to new entrepreneurs, 
particularly technologists and professionals, intending to 
start small and medium scale industries. In Fcbniary 1973. 
Government effected certain changes in their Industrial 
Licensing Policy in order to prevent the entry of large under- 
takings into areas that are primarily meant for small, medium 
and new entrepreneurs. Further, the concept of joint sector 
was spelt out to nurture the growth of new entrepreneurs 
through participation of State Governmcnts/State-level insti- 
tutions in the promotion of industrial projects in partnership 
with new entrepreneurs. 

39. The Corporation, in its operations, has been endeavour- 
ing to give practical shape to this declared policy of the 
Government by giving encouragement to new entrepreneurs 
and technologists to set up sound and viable industrial pro- 
jects. Apart from giving financial assistance, the Corporation 
is extending help and guidance to the new entrepreneurs and 
technologists in project preparation. The Corporation’s policy 
is not to reject prolects which initially do not meet its tests 
of vwbility but to try to improve their viability by suggesting 
necossarv modifications and help fill the caps in project plan- 
ning which might come to its notice. The new entrepreneurs 
who receive special attention from the Corporation are thus 
assured of necessary support in planning and formulation of 
their projects, 

17— 409GT/75 


While (he Corporalion is following a policy of encourag- 
ing new entrepreneurs to set up industries, there are practical 
difficulties in making this policy truly effective. One of the 
main problems faced by the new entrepreneurs and techno- 
logists intending to promote industrial projects is the lack of 
resources to he contributed by them as promoters’ cqiii'y. 

40. The question of providing supplementary finance to- 
wards promoters’ equity had been engaging the attention of 
the Corporation for quite some time. This issue was also 
examined by the Committee on Development of Small and 
Medium Entrepreneurs set up by Government in 1973. One 
of the recommendations of the Committee was that the finan- 
cial institutions should not be too rigid in insisting on a 
minimum share capital from an entrepreneur and should be 
prepared to help him to contribute his share capital and hold 
it in trust for him till such time as he is in a position to buy 
it back. Government has accepted this recommendation in 
principle, subject, however, to the availability of adequate 
resources and submission of well-conceived and viable 
schemes. 

41. Recognising the imperative need to assist in all possible 
ways, the new entrants into industry, so that the national ob- 
jective of broadening the entrepreneurial base in industry is 
achieved, the Corporation sponsored during the year the 
Risk Capital Foundation as a Society registered under the 
Societies Registration Act, 1860. The Foundation which 
would be located in New Delhi was inaugurated by Shri 
T. A. Pai; Minister of Industry and Civil Supplies on March 
10, 1975. 

42. The main objective of the E'oundation as envisaged 
would be to provide assistance to new entrepreneurs, parti- 
cularly technologists and professionals, for promoting indus- 
trial projects, by Riving soft loans to enable them to provide 
n part of the equity capital as promoters’ contribution in res- 
ncct of prolects which have been sanctioned financial assis- 
tance bv the Industrial Finance Corporation of India, alone 
or jointly with other all-India financial institutions. 

43. The resources of the Foundation will consist of : 

fa) Interest free loans from IFCI out of the Benevolent 
Reserve Fund, established under Section 32(B) of 
the TFC Act, 1948. 

(b) Amounts received for the purposes of the Founda- 
tion by way of loans, donations, grants, gifts, con- 
tributions from IFCI, Government, financial institu- 
tions, a body corporate or any other sources. 

(c) Loans and grants out of interest differential funds 
arising out of lines of credit in DM to IFCI from 
the Krcditanstalt fur Wiederaufbau of West Ger- 
many, as may be made available to the Corporation 
by the Government of India from time to time. 

The Corporation has approved an initial allocation of 
Rs. 30 lakhs out of its Benevolent Reserve Fund to the 
Foundation to enable it to start functioning. Further alloca- 
tions will be made by the Corporation from time to time. 

Management Development Institute 

44. It may be recalled that the Corporation sponsored the 
Management Development Institute (MDI) in 1973 with the 
twin objectives of (a) providing training in modern manage- 
ment techniques to the personnel of assisted concerns of the 
Corporation, particularly to new entrepreneurs and technolo- 
gists, who, for the first time, promote an industrial project 
with financial assistance from the Corporation (MDT has, 
since extended its services also to other_ concerns in the pri- 
vate, joint and public sectors) and (b) imparting training In 
development banking to the staff of the financial Institutions 
at the State and all-India levels. 

45. MDI started conducting courses from March 1974 . Till 
December 31, 1974, MDI had organised 14 courses in fields 
like general management, personnel management, marketing 
management, financial management development banking and 
in the area of management of specific industries besides one 
programme for the training of the Corporation’s staff. In 
all, 424 participants took part In the 14 programmes which 
were held in different narts of the country.

During the first half of 1975, MDI conducted 10 prog- 
rammes and is organising 16 more eourses during the latter 
half of the year. 

Special Industry Programmes 

46. Included among the programmes conducted by MDI, 
were those which were tailored to the specific needs of the 
Corporation’s assisted concerns and to the requirements of 
industry and business i Q general. In August, 1974 MDI 
organised a course entitled “Management of Sugar Industry: 
with special reference to co-operatives.” This course was 
organised in close coordination with the Corporation, National 
Cooperative Development Corporation and the National Fede- 
ration of Cooperative Sugar Factories Ltd. The course w as 
designed to provide broad management base for effective utili- 
sation of technical knowledge in respect of cane development 
and manufacture of sugar. In general, the training methodo- 
logy aimed at imparting management techniques through case 
studies related to the sugar industry. 

In September 1974 MDI conducted a “Workshop on Sugar- 
cane Development”; this was designed to : 

(a) broaden the awareness for cane development and 
exchange experiences of the sugar factories in im- 
proving the yield and quality of cane; 

(b) acquaint the participants with the latest research on 
sugarcane; 

(c) formulate plans and implementation procedures for 
effective cane extension and development; and 

(d) promote development among Cane Development 
Officers a positive and responsive appreciation of 
their role and responsibility and thus contribute to 
the growth and expansion of the sugar. 

Another course viz., “Management of Cotton Spinning 
Industry", was organised in January 1975. This programme 
was designed in consultation with the All India Federation of 
Cooperative Spinning Mills Ltd., Textile Research Associa- 
tions and experts in the textile industry, 

tn April 1975, a seminar on “Financing and Financial 
Management of Cooperative Spinning Mills” was also organis- 
ed in collaboration with the All Tndia Federation of Co- 
operative Spinning Mills Ltd., Bombay. The" seminar pro- 
vided a forum for an exchange of ideas on the various prob- 
lems being faced in raising funds and also cave an opportu- 
nity to discuss some of the ways for improving mano"cme''l 
of assets and inventory control to make effective use of avail- 
able funds. The guest facility for these courses included 
experts in the respective fields drawn from all over the 
country. 

Development Banking 

47. MDI organised a series of programmes in develop- 
ment banking. The first programme iri (he field of deve- 
lopment banking was a seminar on “Management of Deve- 
lopment Banks" for the chief executives of development 
banks at the State-level and senior officials of all-India finan-

which were discussed were the nromoiional ro'e of develop-

accounting and taxation, legal aspects of development bank- 
ing etc. 

The second programme on development banking entitled 
“General Course on Development Banking" was designed for 
middle level executives of tho State and all-Tndia financial 
institutions and commercial banks. The main focus of Ihis 
course was to eanin the participants with modern techninues 
o f project appraisal and prolect supervision and to provide 
them with a better understanding of the various tasks being 
undertaken by development banks. 

Tbe third programme in the series was on "Legal Aspects 
of Development Banking”. This course provided an oppor- 
tunity to the legal officers of the financial institutions at tbe 
State and all-I n dia levels and also commercial banks to dis-

“IPIIP" PROGRAMMES 

50. Public sector enterprises have been assigned a crucial 
rolo in the industrial development of the country, Further, 
Ihc joint sector concept has given a new dimension to the 
process of industrialisation in the country. These develop- 
ments have placed increasing responsibilities on the officials 
of the State Governments and the State-level developmental 
agencies, engaged as they arc in the promotion of medium 
and large industrial projects in the public and the joint sec- 
tors. They are also closely connected with the projects be- 
ing implemented by entrepreneurs on their own, as the latter 
would have to depend on the State-level financial institutions 
for guidance and financial assistance, as also on the Govern- 
ment for various approvals, incentives, etc. Further, the 
State Governments as well as the Industrial Development 
Corporations at the State-level are also engaged, in some 
cases, in the actual management and opertaion of industrial 
enterprises set up by them. 

5 1 . The Corporation felt that a better understanding of the 
various aspects of project identification, formulation, evalua- 
tion and implementation of industrial proiects on the part 
of the officials at the State-level would greatly facilitate the 
imnlcmentation of industrial development programmes of 'he 
States. At present, there is no agency in the country which 
has organised a comprehensive programme covering these 
various aspects of protect development and implementation 
as also the operational problems invol ved. With this in view, 
the Corporation decided to sponsor programmes on "Identi- 
fication, promotion and Implementation of Industrial Pro- 
jects”, (TPTTP1 for the benefit of officers in the State Govern- 
ments and State-level develonmental agencies as also entre- 
preneurs. These programmes arc being conducted on behalf 
of Ihc Corporation bv tho Management Development Insti- 
tute. The first such Programme was organised at Puri in 
Orissa in February this year. There were 43 participants, 
including nominees of the Orissa State Government and the 
State-level develonmental agencies. The programme covered 
aspects like project promotion, financing of projects, protect' 
appraisal. imnlcmentation and management of proiects. It is 
proposed to hold the next TPTTP programme, nt Shi'long for 
the Nor*h-enstern States from October 3. 1975 to October 9. 
1975. Similar programmes in other parts of the country will 
be held in the coming months in consultation with the State 
Governments concerned.

Fertilisers 

At the end of 1974, the total installed capacity for nitrogen 
and P«Os stood at 19.81 lakh tonnes and 5.60 lakh tonnes 
respectively, The production of nitrogen during 1974 was 
aooui li.vi lakh tonnes and that of pnosphatic fertilisers 
3.22 lakh tonnes. 

The prices of the main nitrogenous fertilisers viz., urea, 
calcium ammonium mtrate and ammonium sulphate, were in- 
creased from June 1, 1974 to compensate the manufacturers 
for the increase in the cost of napneha, fueloil, bags and 
freight. Government also evolved an equated price lor the 
sale of indigenous manufactured and imported nitrogenous 
fertilisers by which a part of the delivered price was io be 
mopped up by Government through a pool equalisation 
charge towards meeting the cost of imported fertilisers. 

The Kalol plant of the Indian Farmers Fertiliser Coopera- 
tive Ltd., assisted by the Corporation went on stream in 
November 1974 for the production of ammonia, urea and 
NPK. One concern manufacturing urea, could not utilise its 
capacity fully duo to power shortage and operational prob- 
lems. The production of another assisted concern manuiac- 
turing single super-phosphate was affected due to high raw 
material price and power shortage. 

Soda Ash 

There are four units in production in the country. The 
total installed capacity of these units was 6.18 lakh tonnes. 
Production registered an increase by 37,000 tonnes from 
4.69 lakh tonnes in 1973 to 5.06 lakh tonnes in 1974. Pro- 
duction was, however, affected by power cuts and the difficult 
position in the availability of hard coke and sweet water. 

One of our assisted concerns which had increased its capa- 
city from 2.16 lakh tonnes to 3.60 lakh tonnes in 1974 could 
utilise only 75% of its capacity owing to shortage of raw 
materials and of wagons for movement of the finished pro- 
ducts. 

Cement 

There was a decline in tho production of cement in the 
country during 1974 as compared to 1973. Tho production 
of cement in 1974 amounted to 142.6 lakh tonnes as com- 
pared to 148.9 lakh tonnes in the previous year. The total 
installed capacity of the 51 units in production in the country 
aggregated 198.6 lakh tonnes, the percentage utilisation of 
capacity in 1974 being about 72%. 

One of our assisted concerns utilised its capacity fully. 
But most of the assisted concerns worked below capacity and 
their average capacity utilisation was 74%. Better capacity 
utilisation was inhibited by power shortage, interruptions in 
wagon supplies, non-availability of coal, shortage of furnace 
oil etc., as also operational problems. 

Paper 

There were 68 paper mills in operation in (he country 
with an installed capacity of 9,92 lakh tonnes in 1974, The 
production of paper and paper boards was of the order of

Automobile Tyres & Tubes 

'there arc 10 units engaged in the manufacture of auto- 
momie tyres and tunes wun an installed capacity of 62.81 
iakn nos. and 39.91 lakh nos. respectively. Tne estimated 
production of the tyres and tubes dunng J974 was 44.36 takh 
nos. and 38.57 iakii nos. respectively. 

One of our assisted concerns started commercial produc- 
tion of automobile tyres and tubes in November 1974. It 
has now a capacity to produce 4.55 lakh nos. of automobile 
tyres and tubes per atmum. Another assisted concern winch 
has two units could not utilise its capacity due to power 
shortage and market limitations. Another concern’s capacity 
utilisation has been low due to various factors, such as, 
operational problems, market limitations, power shortage and 
shortage of furnace oil. 

Agricultural Tractors 

There are 10 units manufacturing agricultural tractors in 
the range of 25 HP to 75 HP with a total installed capacity 
of 48,000 nos. The production of tractors stood at 29,052 
during 1974. 

The utilisation of capacity of one of our assisted concerns 
was only 57% due to power shortage and shortage of raw 
materials, such as alloy steel. Another concern which is in 
tho State sector having complete indigenous design and know- 
how started commercial production in April 1974. Its capa- 
city was under-utilised due to irregular power supply and 
shortage of raw materials such as steel, forgings, castings, 
engines etc. Both the concerns experienced difficulty in get- 
ting tyres and tubes. 

Cotton Textile 

As against a total of 689 mills with an installed capacity 
of 184.89 lakh spindles and 2.06 lakh looms in 1973, there 
were 691 mills with an installed capacity of 188.57 lakh 
spindles and 2,07 lakh looms at the end of 1974. The pro- 
duction of cotton yam and cloth stood at 10,070 lakh kgs 
and 43,164 lakh metres respectively in 1974. 

Among the important changes introduced by Government 
during 1974 in the scheme of controlled cloth is that tho 
quantum of controlled cloth has been increased from 400 
million square metres to 800 million square metres in mediumi 
and coarse categories. Since October 1974 the mills are re- 
quired to produce controlled categories of cloth to the extent 
of 30% of their production. 

The performance of our assisted concerns was generally 
satisfactory and some of them utilised their capacity fully. 
One of the reasons for under-utilisation of capacity with 
most, was power shortage. The other causes included market 
resistance, operational problems, unsatisfactory industrial 
relations etc. 

Iron & Steel 

There are 12 units in the production of malleable iron 
castings with a total installed capacity of 25,720 tonnes per 
annum. The production of snch castings during 1974 was 
20,000 tonnes.

Sugar 

Production of sugar during the 1974-75 season was about 
47,63 lakn tonnes (till August 1, 1975). Having regard lo 
me continuing need for augmenting sugar production, Gov- 
ernment, mter-alia, took the following important steps for tnc 
1974-75 season. 

(1) Basic minimum cane price for .the 1974-75 season was 
fined at Rs. 8,50 per quintal linked to a recovery of 8,5 per 
cent or below with a premium of 10 paise per every 0.1 per 
cent increase in recovery above 8,5 per cent. 

(2) By suitably amending the Sugarcane (Control) Order, 
1966, the excess realisation accruing to the industry from the 
sale of free sugar was Jo be shared between industry and the 
growers equally by way of additional cane price. 

(3) The policy of partial de-control was continued and the 
proportion of levy sugar to free sale sugar was fixed at 65 : 
33 for the 1974-75 season, 

(4) Suitable rebates in excise duty were announced to in- 
crease sugar production. 

As on December 31, 1974 the total number of cooperative 
sugar factories licensed/registered was 178. Of this 95 
cooperative sugar factories were in production during the 
1974-75 season. The share of the cooperative sector in total 
sugar production of 1974-75 season was 20.81 lakh tonnes 
(till August 1, 1975) constituting about 44% of total produc- 
tion. 

The performance of most of the assisted sugar cooperatives 
of the Corporation was by and large, satisfactory. tyffiny 
cooperatives, particularly in Maharashtra, worked to full "capa- 
city. The sugar recovery was also relatively higher than 
those obtained in the past few years. Those units which 
could not utilise their capacity fully were affected by in- 
adequate cane supplies. 

Synthetic Fibres 

The synthetic fibre industry in the country faced difficulty 
in procuring pfitro-chemical raw materials such as caprolac- 
tuffi. Further, some of the schemes which were approved 
made slow progress. The assisted concerns of the Corpora- 
tion fared well, though they could not utilise the capacity 
fully due to difficult supply position of caprolactum and 
polyester chips. 

Jute 

The revival of demand for jute goods which was witnessed 
in the latter half of 1973 was not sustained. The industry 
faced competition from synthetics as a result of which the ex- 
port demand fell in the second half of 1974. The perform- 
ance of the assisted concerns in the jute industry was not very 
satisfactory, since production was affected by power shortage 
and inadequate availability of raw jute. Consequently, the 
prices of raw jute rose which affected the profitability of the 
assisted concerns.

follow-up once the loan is sanctioned. This is necessary in 
order to find out if there is any deviation from the terms and 
conditions stipulated in the loan ugroement and whether flic 
project is progressing according to schedule or nor.

tor the purposes for which it was sanctioned; 

(ii) During the construction stage, to assess whether 
the progress of construction is proceeding according 
to schedule; 

(iii) To assess whether the project will be completed 
within the original estimates of capital cost; if no,, 
to what extent there is likely to be an over-run; 

(iv) Production performance and assessment of working 
results; 

(v) Efficiency of management; 

(vi) Regularity in submission of progress reports and 
returns; 

(vii) Special problems pertaining to any particular in- 
dustry. 

Procedures 

54. The follow-up procedures devised by the Corporation 
comprise ,tho following : 

(i) Obtaining regular half-yearly progress reports on 
the forms prescribed; 

(ii) Carrying out site inspections of the factory and 
books of account of the assisted concerns at frequent 
intervals; 

(iii) Examining half-yearly /yearly statements of working 
results and financial position of the assisted con- 
cerns; and 

(iv) Appointing, in suitable cases, official/non-official 
nominees on the assisted concerns’ boards to watch 
the interests of the Corporation and to report deve- 
lopments, if any, from time to time in regard to the 
operations and management of the company. 

Progress Reports 

55. Special forms have been devised for submission of 
progress reports, both during the construction and operation 
periods^ keeping in view the special features of »ach industry. 
These forms have been evolved on the basis of the experience 
of the Corporation which shows that the main problems faced 
by an assisted concern during the construction stage relate to 
the arrangement of finance, delays in implementation, over- 
runs in costs, beyond initial estimates and deficiencies in 
management. Apart from enabling the Corporation to ad- 
vise the concern to take remedial steps, the progress reports 
help the Corporation to disburse funds for the project in 
keeping with .the progress achieved and the financial plan. 

After completion of the project, the assisted concerns arc 
normally required, until the loan is repaid in full, to submit 
half-yearly progress reports. 

II is the responsibility of the various offices of the Corpo- 
ration to obtain regularly the progress reports aid take up 
necessary follow-up action. The reports arc examined and 
any adverse fealures/irregulftrities noticed aro brought to the 
notice of the assisted concerns directly by the office concerned 
Under intimation to the Head Office of the Corporation. 



Par* III— Sec. 4] THE GAZETTE OE INDIA, JANUARY 10, 1976 (PAUSA 20, i897) 619 


inspections 

56. From ,the date the agreement is executed and so Jang 
as .any pait ot the xoan remains outstanding, the Corporation 
carnes out site inspections born during sne construction andi 
opei ation periods ot me project and inspects Books 01 atwum 
ot tne assisted concerns. ouch inspections are carried out 
generally oy teams ot nnanciat and lecnmcal officers of the 
corpoiation. A reasonable notice ot about 10/15 days is 
oramanly given to an assisted concern of the proposed ins- 
pection ana lor its preparing and lurmshing tne particulars 
and data for inspection. 10 facilitate proper inspection the 
assisted concerns are required to maintain records showing 
tne expenditure incurred on the project, utilisation ot the 
disbursements out ot 1FC1 loan, progress of the project and 
the operations and financial wonting of the company, in 
carrying out technical and financial inspections, the omcers of 
the corporation visit the borrowers factory, examine relevant 
records and accounts and also schedules, cost estimates, plans 
and specmcations ot the plant, etc. In these inspections, em- 
pnasis is more on discussing the matters and affairs personally 
wnn tne concerned officials ot the company and seeking 
necessary clarifications from them. 

Further, the inspection team satisfies itself that the princi- 
pal sum of loan received from the Corporation is kept in a 
separate bank account, and strictly utilised tor the purpose 
for which it has been sanctioned. All releases of the loan 
amount are pmceded or followed by a pnysical verification 
of the utilisation of the loan amount already disbursed and 
the progress made by the assisted concern towards the im- 
plementation of the project. 

Where other public financial institutions are also involved, 
reports in regard to periodical inspections, unless carried out 
jointly, are mutually exchanged. Reports are forwarded in 
me case of sub-loans in foreign currencies to the respecuv. 
foreign financial institutions, wherever necessary. 

Apart from obtaining periodical progress reports and 
carrying out ot inspections, the assisted concerns are also 
required to send the annual audited balance sheet and cir- 
culars and minutes ot share-holders’ meetings to the Cor- 
poration. The financial statements are carefully studied and 
analysed to assess their progress, profitability and other finan- 
cial aspects compared with the performance ot at least me 
preceeding 3 years. In such an examination apart from draw- 
ing conclusions in regard to the overall performance ot Urn- 
company, the unusual features or breach of covenants with 
IFCI, if any, are also examined. 

Advisory Services 

57. The Advisory Services Department established at Head 
Office of the Corporation in 1973 has been rendering advice 
and guidance ,to new entrepreneurs and others in the techni- 
cal and financial fields both in the pre-implementation and 
post-implementation stages of their projects. Besides, the 
Department gives close attention to the complex area of the 
COiporation’s activities relating to the rehabilitation of pro- 
ject, which run into difficulties or develop symptoms of sick- 
ness for one reason or the other. An indepth analysis of the 
malaise in each case is made by financial and technical ex- 
perts and corrective steps are taken or solution devised and 
adopted in concert with other financial institutions/banks in- 
volved in the project. 

Nominee Directors 

58. An important feature in the building-up of relationship 
between IFCI and the management of assisted concerns is 
the appointment of its nominees on their boards of directors. 
In pursuance of Section 25(2) of the IFC Act, the Corpora- 
tion, as a matter of policy, reserves for itself the right to 
appoint two directors on the board of an industrial concern 
assisted by it. In the case of joint financing, the practice has 
also emerged of having one or more common nominees of 
the participating institutions, where agreed upon. 

IFCI is exercising the right to nominee its representatives 
on the boards of all assisted concerns where substantial finan- 
cial assistance has been sanctioned and/or where the condi- 
tions for conversion of loans into equity have been stipulated 


m tne agreements for financial assistance. IFCI also uses its 
uisociiou m nominating directors on tne boards oi assisted 
wo„cerns generally under tne toliowmg circumstances : 

0 ) wnere the Corporation’s commitments are com- 
paratively large; 

(li) where defaults have been made in the payment of 
principal and/or interest on the Corporation's Iqan; 

(,iu) where tnere are otherwise special circumstances 
calling tor vigilance or a closer watch on tne opera- 
tion.-, ot the assisted concern. 

The persons nominated as directors are either IFCl’s own 
officers or non-officials; the latter generally being experts. 

Tne persons nominated as directors by IFCI hold office 
dm mg us pleasure and are not liable to hold any qualincauon 
snaies or io retirement by rotation. Tne nominee directors 
aie expected to taKe active part in all deliberations ot the 
uoard meetings ot the assisted concerns. Without interfering 
in tne day-to-day management, the nominee directors are 
expected to participate in the discussions on all matters com- 
ing up at tne Board meetings, specially those which have a 
bearing on the assistance given by IFCI and affect its inte- 
rests or are otherwise important as matters of public policy. 

To enable the nominee directors to keep themselves in 
touen with the operations of the concern in matters like pro- 
duction in relation to installed capacity, sales, reasonableness 
ot inventories and receivables, liquidity ot the concern, meet- 
ing ot statutory obligations, changes in key personnel etc., 
protorma have been devised on industry-wise basis for facili- 
tating reporting of certain information and operational data 
oy tne companies for consideration at every meeting of the 
Board of Directors. 

Conversion of Loans into Equity 

59. In accordance with the guidelines issued by Govern- 
ment, the Corporation is reserving the right of conversion ot 
a part of the loans given by it into equity capital ot the 
assisted concerns and the rationale behind this policy has now 
gained fuller acceptance. The Corporation, as on /tine 30, 
lJ'IS, had stipulated the conditions relating to conversion o. 
rupee loans into equity in respect of 180 concerns covering 
214 cases of sanction (loan agreements executed in respect or 
142 cases) after prior negotiations with the sponsors of the 
projects and in consultation with IDB1. The conversion right 
was actually exercised in the case ot one concern as on June 
30, 1975; in ,the case of others, either the time for the exer- 
cise of the right had not yet arrived or it was considered 
expedient taking into account all relevant factors, to wait for 
some time more. 

It will, therefore, be seen .that the relationship between a 
financial institution and an assisted concern is a business 
relationship and it is but natural that the Corporation snoa.u 
take steps no.t only to protect and safeguard its interests, but 
also to offer advisory services. The Corporation, because of 
its considerable financial stake becomes a partner in the 
enterprise and is, therefore, deeply interested in its operations. 

The follow-up procedures call for information which any 
prudent management would collect and study in its own 
interest. As such, the follow-up procedures are not burden- 
some but, on the contrary, are an aid to efficient manage- 
ment. 

Progress of Repayments 

60. The number of defaulting concerns in the engineering 
group of industries was 40 as against 26 in .the previous year. 
Shortage of power continued to be the main factor afflicting 
the working of these units. Some of the units also faced 
shortage of aluminium, the production of which was itself 
affected by power shortage. Other factors which contributed 
to the unsatisfactory position in the engineering industry 
were rising costs of production due to inflationary conditions, 
inefficient management in some cases and difficult working 
capital position. During the latter part of 1974, some of the 
units also faced market resistance caused by the rise in sell- 
ing prices and the credit squeeze. 

The number of defaulting concerns in the sugar industry 
went up from 11 to 16. The performance of these concerns 
was unsatisfactory as the supply of sugarcane was adversely 
affected by drought conditions, lack of irrigation facilities, 
inadequate attention to systematic and planned cane develop- 
ment measures supported by appropriate cane development 
organisation. 


620 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA, JANUARY 10, 1976 (PAUSA 20, 1897) [Part HI— Sec. 4 


Tn the aluminium industry, one of the concerns engaged in 
the manufacture of aluminium ingots and fabrication there- 
from has remained closed since September 1973 due to 
strained industrial relations. 

One of the paper projects which was closed, was success- 
fully rehabilitated, during the pendency of sale procedrings m 
a Court by bringing about a change of management. The 
project has since resumed production. 

The number of defaulting concerns in the cotton textile 
industry increased from 12 to 20 during the year. Shortage 
of power and sluggish market conditions in the textile industry 
during the year alfected the proper functioning of the textile 
units. Most of the concerns which were in default during 
the previous year could not meet their commitments during 
the current year also. One of the closed textile units which 
was rehabilitated by change of management at the Corpora- 
tion’s instance through the intervention of the Court has 
since gone into production. 

In respect of nine textile units financed by the Corpora- 
tion, which were nationalised under the Sick Textile Mills 
(Nationalisation) Act, 1974, the compensation stipulated 
under the said Act was found to be inadequate to meet the 
entire dues of the Corporation, particularly, in view ot the 


relatively low priority of the Corporation’s dues in the 
seneme of distribution of compensation money envisaged 
under the said Act. The Corporation, with a view to pro- 
tecting its interests has approached the Central Government 
and it is understood that certain measures for safeguarding 
the dues of the banks/financial institutions are under con- 
sideration of Government. 

The number of defaulting concerns in tho jute industry 
went up from 2 to 3 during the year. This was largely due 
to shortage of power and depressed export market, 

three concerns in the coal mining industry assisted by the 
Corporation, were nationalised. The Corporation may not be 
able to realise its entire outstanding dues out of tho compen- 
sation fixed for each of the three coal units. However, the 
Corporation has filed claims before the Commissioner of 
Payments appointed under the relevant Nationalisation Acts 
for payment of its dues out of the compensation money in 
terms of tho said Acts. 

During the year, some of the projects were granted addi- 
tional financial assistance as part of the overall schemes of 
their rehabilitation, in participation with other institutions, 
accompanied by reliefs by way of roll-over of the loans, and

RESOURCES . 

62. The internal resources of the Corporation for meeting 
its commitments in respect of disbursement of assistance 
sanctioned and repayment of borrowirigs frbm the Central 
Government and foreign credit institutions as also redemption 
of bonds from year to year comprise mainly additions to 
reserves, repayment of loans by assisted concerns, sale of 
investment and redemption of debentures/preference shares; 
other sources for meeting the said commitments are borrow- 
ings from the market by way of Bond Issues and borrowings 
from foreign credit institutions. There has been uo budge- 
tary allocation to the Corporation from the Central Govern- 
ment for the last 6 years (except small amounts' on account 
of interest differential funds arising out -of KfW lines of credit 
and amounts made available under the Hotel Development 
Scheme), The Corporation has bad. however, to renav 
Central Government loans obtained earlier as also loans 
raised from foreign credit institutions and has had to redeem 


bonds issued in the past, according to fixed schedules and 
the quantum of such repayments are increasing year by year, 
Thfc annual increase in internal resources and the net in- 
crease in borrowing have thus not been adequate to support 
any substantial increase in disbursements from year to year. 
As such, the growth of disbursement of assistance is largely 
circumscribed by the extent of market borrowings that may 
be permitted to the Corporation from year to year. Al- 
though during the year under review, the Corporation was 
able to secure for the first time, ad-hoc accommodation from 
LIC as mentioned earlier and accordingly could make addi- 
tional disbursements therefrom, the Corporation faced con- 
siderable stringency in regard to resources in view of the in- 
creased demand for funds vis-a-vis restricted market borrow- 
ings.

the Corporation stood at Rs. 10 crores as on June 30, 1975. 

There has been no chance in th» ownership pattern of 
shares of the Corporation held bv the various categories of 
shareholders during the year under report.

Borrowings from the Central Government 

65. As on June 30, 1974, loans outstanding from the 
Central Government stood at Rs. 66.06 crores. During the 
year under review, a sum of Rs. 0,21 crorc was made avail- 
able as loan by Government under interest differential funds 
arising out of KfW loans and a further sum of Rs. 1.59 
crores was borrowed from Government under the Hotel 
Development Scheme for financing hotel projects; a sum of 
Rs. 6.55 crores was repaid during the year. The aggregate 
amount of Government loans outstanding at the end of the 
year was Rs. 61.31 crores.

66. As in the past, borrowings from RBI were availed of 
for temporary periods during the year. As on June 30, 1975 
the outstandings under this head amounted to Rs. 80.50 
lakhs.

thercagainst totalled FF 14.58 million.

ACCOUNTS 

69. The gross profit for the year amounted to Rs. 458.97 
lakhs. After providing Rs. 198.97 lakhs (net) for taxation, 
the net profit amounted to Rs. 260.00 lakhs as against 
Rs. 325.00 lakhs for the year 1973-74. The appropriations 
to reserves amounted to Rs. 199.00 lakhs compared with 
Rs. 264.00 lakhs last year. As in the last year, allocation 
to the Staff Welfare Fund amounted to Rs. 1.00 lakh. 

Dividend 

70. With the transfer of Rs. 75.00 lakhs to the General 
Reserve Fund out of profits for the year, the total amount 
in the Fund exceeded the paid-up share capital of the Cor- 
poration as on June 30, 1975 by 1.75 crores. As permissible 
under Section 32 of the IFC Act as amended, the Corpora- 
tion has declared a dividend of 6% on the paid-up capital 
in respect of the year ended June 30, 1975.

Benevolent Reserve Fund 

A sum of Rs. 3100 lakhs has been transferred out of 
the current years’ profits to the Benevolent Reserve Fund 
under Section 32B of the Industrial Finance Corporation 
Act to be utilised as under : 

(a) for meeting the cost of feasibility studies, project 
reports. ma'ket and techno-economic surveys 
and such other purposes which, in the opinion of 
the Corporation, may promote the development of 
industries; 

(b) in the field of development banking and in finan- 
cial and industrial management — 

(i) for undertaking and promoting research; 

(ii) for training in India or abroad of personnel 
of financial institutions; and 

(iii) for creating chairs in universities, academic in- 
stitutions and research foundations; 

(c) for assisting projects promoted by technologists 
and new entrepreneurs — 

(i) by subsidising the normal lending rate of 
interest of the Corporaton in respect of loans 
or advances sanctioned to them; 

(if) by providing technical and managerial assis- 
tance to projects promoted by them especially 
in industrially less developed regions; 

(d) for rendering any assistance that may be ancillary 
or incidental to the aforementioned purposes. 

The amount to the credit of the Benevolent Reserve Fund 
was Rs. 79t51 lakhs as on June 30, 1975. 

Reserve for Doubtful Debts 

A review of the loan accounts as at the end of the year 
shows a satisfactory position. In view, however, of the 
75. A statement showing the working results for the last 


large size of the operations of the Corporation, and the fact 
that schemes for rehabilitation of certain projects may take 
time to mature, the Directors have decided, as a measure 
of prudence, to transfer an amount of Rs. 50.00 lakhs from 
the profits of the year under report to the Reserve for 
Doubtful Debts which now stands at Rs. 349-80 lakhs. 

Provision for Income-Tax 

73. The assessment proceedings for the accounting years 
ended June 30, 1972 1973 and 1974 (assessment years 
1973-74, 1974-75 and 1975-76) were not finalised by the 
close of the annual accounts. Tn respect of the accounting 
year ended June 30, 1975 a sum of Rs. 198.97 lakhs (after 
adjusting refund of Rs. 15.49 lakhs in respect of earlier 
years) has been provided in the accounts for taxation.

in terms of Section 10(1) (a) of the IFC Act, 1948, 
the Central Government appointed Shri Baldev Pasricha, 
General Manager, as the Chairman of the Corporation, vide 
Notification No, F. 2(60)lF,l/74 dated March 25, 1975. 
Shri Pasricha assumed charge of the office of the Chairman 
with effect from March 31, 1975 (afternoon). 

Other Directors 

In terms of Section 10(1) (aa) of the IFC Act, 1948, 
the Industrial Development Bank o£ India nominated Sbri 
C. T. Das and Prof. D. T. Lakdawala as Directors in place 
of Shri F. K. F. Nariman and Dr. Samuel Paul respectively 
on December 11, 1974. 

The Board place on record their appreciation of the 
valuable services rendered by Shri F. K. F. Nariman and 
Dr. Samuel Paul, while they were associated with the Cor- 
poration, and extended a hearty welcome to the new 
Directors, 

Meetings oj ike Board and other Committees 

77. Twelve meetings of the Board were held during the 
year, five in New Delhi and one each at Bangalore, Bombay 
Bhopal, Madras, Ahmedabad, Bhubaneswar and Cochin. 

These meetings of Committees of the Board were also 
held during the year.

The Corporation continued to maintain a panel of tech-
