Auditors 

79. M/s. Ray &. Ray, Calcutta, were re-appointed by the 
Industrial Development Bank of India as auditors of the 
Corporation for* the year ended June 30, 1975. At the 
Annual General Meeting ol’ the shareholders of the Cor- 
poration held on September 26, 1974, M/s. Haribhakti & 
Co., Bombay, were elected auditors by the shareholders, 
other than 1DB1, for the same period. M/s. Haribhakti & 
Co. will retire at the end of the year but are eligible for 
re-election. 

Progressive Use of Hindi in the Corporation 

80. In pursuance of Government’s policy for the pro- 
gressive use of Hindi for official purposes, the Corporation 
is making efforts to promote the use of Hindi in the Cor- 
poration. As in the previous years, the Corporation has 
brought out the Hindi version of the Annual Report on 
the working of the Corporation for the year ended June 
30, 1974. Two Official Languages Implementation Com- 
mittees, one at Head Office and another at Bombay Region- 
al Offige are also functioning to watch the progress and to 
suggest steps for the progressive use of Hindi in the Cor- 
poration. The meetings of these Committees are held once 
in a quarter, Hindi Teaching classes are also being con- 
ducted under the Hindi Teaching Scheme of the Ministry 
of Home Affairs for the benefit of the staff at Head Office 
and the Delhi Regional Office. Steps are also underway 
to extend Hindi teaching facilities to the staff at the other 
offices of the Corporation. The obligatory provisions of. 
Official Languages Act, 1963, are being complied with in 
so far as they relate to pur Corporation and accordingly. 
Gazette notifications, press communiques, notices, advertise- 
ments are being released both in Hindi and English. All 
letters received in Hindi arc replied to in Hindi. Billingual 
letter heads are used at all the offices of the Corporation. 
IFCI Silver Jubilee Memorial Lecture 

81. To commemorate the Corporation’s Silver Jubilee, 
the Corporation instituted the TFCI Silver Jubilee Memorial 
Lecture’. 


The second Lecture was delivered by Mr. William Dia- 
mond, Director, South Asia Department, International Bank 
tor Reconstruction and Development, Washington on Oc- 
tober 16, 1974. The subject ol Mr. Diamond’s Lecture 
was ‘The Inter-Play Beiween Financial inuuuuons and 
Economic Development”, Shri H. T. Parekh, Chairman, 
Industrial Credit and Investment Corporation of India Ltd., 
presided over the function. The Lecture was attended by 
a large gathering of distinguished bankers, industrialists, 
economists, administrators and foreign dignitaries. 

Training of Personnel 

82. The Corporation is paying increasing attention to 
various aspects of executive development. During the year, 
58 officers of the Corporation attended courses conducted 
by various well-known institutions in the country, such as 
the Management Development Institute, New Delhi, Bankers 
Training College, Bombay, National Productivity Council, 
New Delhi, etc., as compared to 30 officers in the previous 
year. One officer attended a course on “Development Bank- 
ing" conducted by the Economic Development Institute of 
the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development 
at Washington. Anotncr officer attended a course on “Project 
Appraisal and Fqllow-up for Development Bankers” con- 
ducted by the United fictions Asian institute for Economic 
Development and Planning, Bangkok. ln-company pro- 
grammes were also organised for the officers at Head Office, 
A workshop for the staff of the Calcutta Regional Office 
was also organised. 

Staff Welfare Fund 

83. The rules, of the Staff Welfare Fund were amended 
during the year in order to provide for assistance to the 
children of deceased, employees to pursue their studies 
through grants from the Staff Welfare Fund. Further, the 
children on staff have been made eligible subject to certain 
conditions for scholarships for prosecuting technical studies 
after passing middle school/school leaving certmca.e exami- 
nations. Scholarships are also granted to the children of 
staff for prosecuting technical professional and other courses 
viz., Degree courses in medicine, engineering, architecture 
and post-graduate courses in Science, Commerce, Business 
Administration and Business Management. A number of 
Holiday Homes have been established by the Corporation 
in different parts of the country for the use of its staff. 
In order to enable the Staff Recreation Clubs to render 
better service, provision has been made to augment the re- 
sources of these clubs by grants from the Staff Welfare 
Fuud to finance their welfare activities. 

Changes in Senior Management 

84. Shri R. B, Mathur was promoted as General Mana- 
ger and Shri M. S. Nagratha as Deputy General Manager 
with effect from April 29, 1975. On attaining the age of 
superannuation, Shn Mathur retired from the service on 
August 9, 1975 but has been re-employed as General 
Manager for a period of one year, in the first instance, 
from August 10, 1975. Shri T. M. Sen, Legal Adviser, 
having regard to his special status, has been ranked for 
purposes of protocol next to Chairman. 

Shri P. S. Gopalakrishnan was promoted to the post of 
Assistant General Manager with effect from June 19, 1975. 
Sarvashri 1, S. N’angia, D. N- Davar and N. P. Chakraborty 
were also promoted as Assistant General Managers with 
effect from August 2, 1975. Shri Nangla will, however, 
continue to hold charge of the Bombay Regional Office. 

Dr. J. C. Rao was promoted as Economic Adviser with 
effect Irom August 2, 1975. 

Acknowledgement of Assistance Received 

85. The Board wish to place on record their appreciation 
of the cooperation and assistance received from the various 
Ministries and Departments of the Government of India and 
the all-India financial institutions. The Board are grateful 
to the members, who have served on the various Advisory 
Committees of the Corporation for their valuable assis- 
tance and advice, and also to the non-officials, who have 
served as the Corporation's nominees on the Boards of 
Directors of the various assisted concerns. The Board also 
wish to express their appreciation for the loyal and devoted 
service put in by the officers and the staff of the Corpora- 
tion during the year. 

On behalf of the Directors 
BALDEV PASRICHA 
Chairman

1. No provision has been made for depreciation in the value of investments held by the Corporation because it is felt by the 
Corporation that such depreciation is a normal incident of the business of a development bank. 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA, JANUARY 10, 1976 (PAUSA 20, 1897) [Part 111— Sec. 4 


Coutd, 

2. Investments under Section 23 (1) (d) include a sum of Rs. 1,97,900/- in the equity share cupiLal of a company which has 
gone into voluntary liquidation and the Corporation is not likely to realise the full amount invested. No specific provi- 
sion has been made in respect thereof. 

3. Loans and Advances do not include Rs. 9,09,38,000/- in respect of which the rights and interests oT I he Corporation have 
been transferred under S. 2IB of the Act against amount received in cash. 

4. An aggregate amount of Rs. 340,77,850/- was due on the date of the Balance Sheet from certain coal and coke mining 
and textile companies, the undertakings of which have boon acquired by tho Central Governments. It has not been 
possible to determine as to what portion of the said amount can be recovered either out of the compensation or from the 
guarantors. After taking into account the further amount of Rs. 50,00,000/- transferred to ‘Reserve for Doubtful Debts’ 
this year, it is considered that the ‘Reserve for Doubtful Debts’ and the amount standing to the credit of ‘Interest held in 
Suspense A;eounr arc sufficient on a net of tax basis to cover the doubtful loans, advances and Sundry Debtors, 

5. In accordance with tho past practice, the figures of foreign currency loans availed and sub-loans granted to sub-borrowers 
are arrived at by conversion into Rupees of all foreign currencies except pound sterling at the erstwhile IMF parity rates 
viz., S 1 -00-Rs. 7 '50, DM 48 -80-Rs. 100/-, FF 1. 00=Rs. 1 -35. The rupeo equivalent of pound sterling is arrived 
at by ‘ converting the same at the composite rate of £1 '00-=Rs. 19 -06. If the foreign currency balancos arc converted 
into Rupees at theT.T. selling rates ruling on 30th June, 1975, the Loans and Advances m foreign currency and the Borrow 
ings from foreign credit institutions would be Rs. 40 -78 crores and Rs. 38 -07 crores respectively. 

6. ‘Commitment and other charges accrued’ include a sum of Rs. 65,185/- due from three concerns which is considered doubt- 
ful of recovery and is held in Suspense. 

7. 'Sundry Debtors’ include a sum of Rs, 1,25,00,000/- being the amount due from a State Government in respect of the 
sale price of shares of an assisted concern sold to them under a Rehabilitation Scheme, The sale proceeds arc receivable 
in six annual instalments commencing from 1976. 

8. On a review of the question of Difference in Exchange inrespect of foreign currency loans it has been decided that all 
the balancos arising out of difference in exchange rates so far classified under the heads “Sundry Debtors”, “Sundry 
Creditors” and “Difference in Exchange" be aggregated under the head "Difference in Exchange Suspense Account” 
to be adjusted later, 

9. No provision has been made in respect of liability for future gratuity payable to members of the staff (amount not 
ascertained). 

10. Previous year’s figures have been regrouped wherever necessary.

INDUSTRIAL FINANCE CORPORATION OF INDIA 
REPORT OF THE AUDITORS 


TO THE SHAREHOLDERS 

OF THE INDUSTRIAL FINANCE CORPORATION OF INDIA 

We, the undersigned Auditors of the Industrial Finance Corporation of India, do hereby report to the Shareholders upon the 
Balance Sheet and Accounts of the Corporation as at 30th June, 1973. 

We have examined the attached Balance Sheet with the Accounts and Vouchers relating thereto and the audited returns from the 
Branches, which returns are incorporated In the above Balance Sheet, and report that where we have called for explanations and In- 
formation, such information and explanations have been given and have been satisfactory. In our opinion, the Balance Sheet together 
with the notes thereon is a full and fair Balance Sheet containing all necessary particulars and properly drawn up in accordance with the 
Act and the Rules of the Corporation so as to exhibit a true and correct view of the state of the affairs of the Corporation according to 
the beet of our information and explanations given to us and as shown by the books of the Corporation. 


New Delhi 

Dated 38th August, 1975. 


HARIBHAKTI & CO. 

RAY & RAY 
Chartered Accountants

APPENDIX H 

TERMS OF CONCESSIONAL FINANCE 
(As on June 30, 1975) 

With a view to providing greater inducement to entrepreneurs to spread out in relatively under-developed areas in the country, 
the Industrial Finance Corporation of India (IFC1), in supersession of its earlier announcements made in July 1970 and January 1972, 
has dscidod to liberalise and enlarge the scope of the scheme of financial assistance on concessional terms to industrial projects in such 
areas. The scheme would n 0 w cover all industrial projects — new, expansion or rehabilitation in the corporate as well as cooperative 
sectors, irrespective of their capital cost. The principal features of the revised scheme of concessional finance for units in identified less 
developed districts/areas would be as under : 

1. Location : 

All industrial projects located/to be located in the districts in the various States or Union Territories selected for such assistance 
by the Central Government from time to time would be eligible for assistance on concessional terms. 

2. Scope of the Scheme : 

Concessional finance would be extended to all industrial projects — new and expansion— both in the corporate (limited companies) 
as well as cooperative sectors. Concessional terms would also be made applicable to assistance granted by the Corporation by way of 
rehabilitation finance to units located in notified less developed districts/arcas on the same basis as applicable to new and expansion 
projects. 

3. Ceiling on Assistance : 

T i ; a /j.uU c filing in respect of loans extended on concessional terms from the Corporation individually, unless other institutions 
also n irtioipitj a Tj extend concessional finance upto Rs. 2 -00 crorcs on a pro rata basis, has been fixed at Rs, 1 >00 crore. The sum of 
Rs. 1 -03 crore would, however, include outstanding rupee assistance, if any, already granted on concessional terms. Underwriting 
at fist lice from the term fiunsiig institutions including the Corporation would be made available at concessional terms upto a ceiling of 
Rs. 1 -00 crore in the aggregate, irrespective of the cost of the project. 

4. Terms : 

(i) Rate of Interest 

As against the normal current rate ofintcrest on rupceloans at 11 '25% and on foreign currency loans at 11 -50% (with a rebate 
of 1 % for punctual payments of instalments of interest and principal) lower rate of interest viz., 9 -50 % and 10 -50 % respectively (with 
a rebate of 1 %) would be charged under the scheme. 

(it) Period of repayment of loans 

Tnj Corporation’s normal practice is to allow initial moratorium upto 3 years to an assisted concern before the first repayment 
of the principal amount of the loan com nonces. In the case of undertakings in the less developed districts/areas, this period would be 
oxtended upto five years from the date of first disbursement of the loan, on a case to case basis, having regard to the projections of 
profitability and ways and moans position of a concern. Likewise, against the normal period allowed for repayment of loans, the period 
in the case of projects coming up in loss developed districts/areas may be extended on the merits of each case having regard to theeoncern’s 
profitability potential and cash flow position, 

(III) Promoters’ contribution and equity-debt ratio 

A lower contribution than usual by promoters to the total project cost and somewhat liberal equity-debt ratio would be con- 
sidered on the merits of each case having regard to the financial status and standing of the promoters, gestation period of a particular 
project, its profitability potential and other relevant factors, 

(tv) Participation in equity and preference capital 

Depending on the merits of each case, the Corporation would be prepared to consider participation by way of underwriting or 
otherwise in the share capital of an industrial concern located in loss d oveloped districts/areas tq> a greater extent as compared to projects 
located elsewhere. 

(v) Reduction in other charges 

In the case of Rupee Loans, 50% reduction would be made in theCorporation’s normal charges in respect of commitment charge, 
non-refundable examination fee for processing of applications and legal charges. Fifty per cent reaction would also be made in the 
Corporation’s normal charges in respect of underwriting commission.
